On based this The Yana Rhinoceros Horn Site (Yana RHS) is an Upper Palaeolithic archaeological site located near the lower Yana river in northeastern Siberia, Russia, north of the Arctic Circle in the far west of Beringia. It was discovered in 2001, after thawing and erosion exposed animal bones and artifacts. The site features a well-preserved cultural layer due to the cold conditions, and includes hundreds of animal bones and ivory pieces and numerous artifacts, which are indicative of sustained settlement and a relatively high level of technological development. With an estimated age of around 32,000 calibrated years before present (cal BP), the site provides the earliest archaeological evidence for human settlement in this region, or anywhere north of the Arctic Circle, where people survived extreme conditions and hunted a wide range of fauna before the onset of the Last Glacial Maximum. The Yana site is perhaps the earliest unambiguous evidence of mammoth hunting by humans.",

The act of giving up a legal right, particularly a right of ownership of property. Property that has been abandoned is res nullius (a thing belonging to no one), and a person taking possession of it therefore acquires a lawful title. An item is regarded as abandoned when it can be established that the original owner has discarded it and is indifferent as to what becomes of it: such an item cannot be the subject of a theft charge. However, property placed by its owner in a dustbin is not abandoned, having been placed there for the purpose of being collected as refuse. In marine insurance, abandonment is the surrender of all rights to a ship or cargo in a case of constructive total loss. The insured person must do this by giving the insurer within a reasonable time a notice of abandonment, by which he relinquishes all his rights to the ship or cargo to the insurer and can treat the loss as if it were an actual total loss. 2. (of debts) The proportionate reduction in the payment of debts that takes place if a person's assets are insufficient to settle with his creditors in full. 2. (of legacies) The reduction or cancellation of legacies when the estate is insufficient to cover all the legacies provided for in the will or on intestacy after payment of the deceased's debts. The Administration of Estates Act 1925 provides that general legacies, unless given to satisfy a debt or for other consideration, abate in proportion to the amounts of those legacies; specific and demonstrative legacies then abate if the estate is still insufficient to pay all debts, and a demonstrative legacy also abates if the specified fund is insufficient to cover it. B will receive the painting. C's demonstrative legacy abates to £300, and after the debts are paid from the remaining £700, D's and E's general legacies abate proportionately, to £480 and £120 respectively. When annuities are given by the will, the general rule is that they are valued the date of the testator's death, then abate proportionately in accordance with that valuation, and each annuitant receives the abated sum. All these rules are subject to any contrary intention being expressed in the will. 3. (in land law) Any reduction or cancellation of money payable. According to the United Nations, India is expected to have over 340 million people over the age of 60 by 2050. This demographic shift poses several challenges, including the need for specialized health care services for the elderly. ASHA workers can play a crucial role in addressing this challenge by providing basic health care services to the geriatric population in their communities.\nASHA workers are trained to provide basic health care services, including maternal and child health care. However, they lack specialized training in geriatric care. The lack of specialized training can lead to poor quality of care for the elderly. This lack of training can lead to poor quality of care for the elderly. The geriatric population has unique health care needs that require specialized training. Therefore, it is essential to train ASHA workers in geriatric care.\nInadequate compensation\nASHA workers are not paid a regular salary but instead receive incentives for their work. These incentives are often inadequate and do not reflect the amount of work that ASHA workers do. This can lead to demotivation among ASHA workers and affect the quality of care they provide.\nLack of recognition\nASHA workers often do not receive recognition for their work. They are often seen as volunteers rather than health care professionals. This lack of recognition can affect their motivation and lead to burnout.\nCultural Beliefs and Practices\nCultural beliefs and practices can affect the delivery of geriatric care. For example, some elderly people may prefer traditional remedies over modern medicine. The program also aims to train health personnel, including ASHA workers, in geriatric care.\nHowever, there are several challenges to integrating ASHA workers into existing government schemes for improving the health of the geriatric population. In addition, there may be a lack of coordination between different government schemes that can lead to duplication of efforts or gaps in service delivery.\nRecommendations\nGiven the challenges discussed above, what are some possible strategies for integrating ASHA workers in geriatric care? There is no one-size-fits-all solution, but here are some ideas that could be considered:\n1.\tTraining and Capacity Building\nThe first step towards integrating ASHA workers in geriatric care is to provide them with appropriate training and capacity building. ASHA workers require specialized training in geriatric care that includes basic principles of aging, age-related diseases, and their management. The training should also cover communication skills, care planning, medication management, and palliative care. ASHA workers should be provided with hands-on training on the use of medical equipment and devices like blood pressure monitors, glucometers, and thermometers. They should also be trained to identify common geriatric syndromes such as falls, delirium, and incontinence. This includes ensuring adequate infrastructure, equipment, and supplies at the primary health care centers (PHCs) and sub-centers. The PHCs should have a well-equipped laboratory for routine blood and urine tests. The sub-centers should have basic equipment like weighing scales, stethoscopes, and sphygmomanometers. The PHCs and sub-centers should also have adequate stocks of essential drugs for geriatric care. They should be given a fixed salary instead of incentives, which is more predictable and provides greater financial security. The recognition could take the form of certificates, awards, and public appreciation events. The recognition should be linked to their performance in geriatric care and could be used as a tool for motivation and performance improvement.\n5.\tCoordination and Collaboration\nThere needs to be coordination and collaboration between different government schemes and programs that focus on improving the health of the geriatric population. The challenges faced by ASHA workers in geriatric care are significant and require a multi-pronged approach. The strategies discussed above are not exhaustive but provide a starting point for policymakers and program managers to formulate an effective strategy towards geriatric health care.”\n",
